I guess what really made me come in is… I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like at work, at the bench, I’ll start answering a customer email, then I notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside and I’m looking up, and then suddenly I’ve lost track of the whole work order.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.

I missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. It was one of those pieces where the customer had been really specific, and I had all these ideas for making it better, and then I was chasing three other things at the same time. My records got all mixed up too—orders, notes, deposits, stone sizes—just scattered. I usually keep that stuff pretty tight, because if you mix up a stone measurement by even a little, the whole setting’s wrong.

I actually had to stop taking new work for now, which feels embarrassing. My family’s been checking on me more too, like calling and stopping by, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I kept telling them I was just busy, but… yeah, it was more than that.

And then I’d get sidetracked looking at lighting for photos because the pictures never show the metal right, especially with silver—it always picks up whatever color is around it. But yeah, the sleep thing was definitely less than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. After the show, especially, I felt kind of lit up, like bigger than myself for a bit. Like everything I’d made was finally clicking and people were seeing it the right way.\n\nAnd then it would flip really fast. If someone asked me to keep normal shop hours or, you know, slow down and not act like the whole place should revolve around whatever idea I had, I’d get sharp with them. Snappy. Even if they weren’t really doing anything wrong. So it wasn’t just happy exactly… it was like happy and electric, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel more confident or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Especially after I won that ribbon, it was like, okay, see, I’ve been right this whole time. I felt like I’d finally proved I was on another level.\n\nI remember looking at the custom orders and thinking I could turn every single one into some showpiece, like something no one else around here could even touch. Which… I mean, some of the local work really is kind of lazy, people just slap a stone in a setting and call it done—but I guess I was feeling more important than usual, yeah.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot when I’m explaining a piece anyway, like if someone asks about a setting I’ll get into why I used a certain prong shape or why the stone sits better lower, and then suddenly I’m talking about polishing compounds or whatever.\n\nSo it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “much more.” People have told me I get going and don’t really stop, but I don’t know if that was those periods specifically or just… me being wound up about the work.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, were you taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I kind of… started trying to spin up everything at once. I was lining up new commissions, messaging old customers like, “Hey, I’ve got new ideas if you want something made,” and then I had this whole plan for a website.\n\nAnd not just a basic website, either. I was thinking it needed sections, photos, maybe a story behind each piece—because people don’t understand how much goes into the metalwork, you know? Like with Appalachian stuff, the patterns in old quilts and rail lines and creek stones, there’s a whole language there.\n\nSo yeah, I was sketching this whole Appalachian collection at the same time, and it felt like I had to get all of it moving right then, before the idea cooled off or somebody else did something similar.\n\nDoctor: During one of those wired periods, did you ever do something that got you into serious trouble at work, financially, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. It was one of those pieces where the customer had been really specific, and I had all these ideas for making it better, and then I was chasing three other things at the same time. My records got all mixed up too—orders, notes, deposits, stone sizes—just scattered. I usually keep that stuff pretty tight, because if you mix up a stone measurement by even a little, the whole setting’s wrong.\n\nI actually had to stop taking new work for now, which feels embarrassing. My family’s been checking on me more too, like calling and stopping by, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I kept telling them I was just busy, but… yeah, it was more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever hear or see things that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing a voice talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes when I’m really locked into a piece, I’ll notice tiny things other people miss—like a little flash in a stone or how the light catches an edge wrong—and I can get kind of fixated on it. My brother will be like, “It looks fine,” and I’m still staring at it under the lamp. But that’s not really hearing or seeing something, I guess. I mean, I talk a lot when I’m explaining a piece anyway, like if someone asks about a setting I’ll get into why I used a certain prong shape or why the stone sits better lower, and then suddenly I’m talking about polishing compounds or whatever.\n\nSo it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “much more.” People have told me I get going and don’t really stop, but I don’t know if that was those periods specifically or just… me being wound up about the work.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, were you taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I kind of… started trying to spin up everything at once. I was lining up new commissions, messaging old customers like, “Hey, I’ve got new ideas if you want something made,” and then I had this whole plan for a website.\n\nAnd not just a basic website, either. I was thinking it needed sections, photos, maybe a story behind each piece—because people don’t understand how much goes into the metalwork, you know? Like with Appalachian stuff, the patterns in old quilts and rail lines and creek stones, there’s a whole language there.\n\nSo yeah, I was sketching this whole Appalachian collection at the same time, and it felt like I had to get all of it moving right then, before the idea cooled off or somebody else did something similar.\n\nDoctor: During one of those wired periods, did you ever do something that got you into serious trouble at work, financially, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. It was one of those pieces where the customer had been really specific, and I had all these ideas for making it better, and then I was chasing three other things at the same time. My records got all mixed up too—orders, notes, deposits, stone sizes—just scattered. I usually keep that stuff pretty tight, because if you mix up a stone measurement by even a little, the whole setting’s wrong.\n\nI actually had to stop taking new work for now, which feels embarrassing. My family’s been checking on me more too, like calling and stopping by, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I kept telling them I was just busy, but… yeah, it was more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever hear or see things that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing a voice talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes when I’m really locked into a piece, I’ll notice tiny things other people miss—like a little flash in a stone or how the light catches an edge wrong—and I can get kind of fixated on it. My brother will be like, “It looks fine,” and I’m still staring at it under the lamp. But that’s not really hearing or seeing something, I guess. So… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did those wired periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Um… the main one was about two weeks, I think. It started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, which was a big deal for me—like, I kept replaying people coming by the table and asking about the pieces, and I had all these ideas after that.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking days in a row, it wasn’t just a weekend or a couple late nights. It was probably around two weeks where I felt really wired and kind of couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I was hospitalized for it. I mean, people were definitely worried, and there was a lot going on with work and my family hovering more than usual, but I didn’t end up in the hospital.\n\nI remember thinking at the time that if I could just get the shop cleaned up and sort the orders, it would all kind of settle down. The little bins on my bench were a mess—gold scraps in the wrong tray, invoices under polishing cloths, just… ridiculous. Yeah… these past couple weeks, I think so. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the shine’s gone out of everything.

Like at work, at the bench, I’ll start answering a customer email, then I notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside and I’m looking up, and then suddenly I’ve lost track of the whole work order.\n\nAnd jewelry work, you know, it’s not really something where you can just be halfway there—one little mistake and the piece is off, or the customer gets upset. My boss has this old radio near the polishing area too, and sometimes that just pulls me off completely. But yeah… mostly it’s that my attention is all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I wasn’t exactly the kid sitting there loving worksheets, but who was? I did okay enough to get through.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, because back then I was more worried about shop class and hanging around after school, and my mom used to say I’d “forget my head if it wasn’t attached,” but she said that about everything—keys, lunch, chores. I don’t know if that’s the same kind of attention problem you mean.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, were you often distracted or forgetful at school or at home?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Like, before 12… that’s kind of fuzzy. I remember forgetting stuff here and there, like homework or my jacket, but I don’t know if it was “often” or just normal kid stuff.\n\nAt home my mom would get on me about chores, but she got on my brother too, so I don’t know. And school feels like a blur now—teachers, lockers, cafeteria pizza, all that. I can’t really say for sure that it was a big problem back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I think everybody worries, right? Like bills, work, whether a customer’s gonna come back mad because a clasp isn’t sitting right—well, not mad, but you know, people can be picky with jewelry.\n\nBut when you say “even when there isn’t a problem,” I’m not sure. I don’t really sit down and label it as worrying. Sometimes my head’s just busy, and I’ll be thinking about three random things, like whether I locked the case at work or if I need gas, then I remember some guy from high school I haven’t talked to in years. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. There’ve been times where I felt really wired for a few days, like I just couldn’t settle down.\n\nAt work, especially, I couldn’t sit still at the bench. I’d start sketching out a new watch case, then suddenly I’d be emailing suppliers, then I’d decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them even after closing. Which is funny because those trays are never right anyway—people move things around all day and then I’m the one fixing it.\n\nBut yeah, it wasn’t just normal busy. It was like I had to keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… during those times I’d sleep way less. Like maybe two or three hours, and then I’d pop up feeling weirdly clear, like I was ready to get started.\n\nI remember getting up before sunrise thinking I should cut stones or post new design ideas online, even though normally I’d be dragging if I slept that little. And then I’d get sidetracked looking at lighting for photos because the pictures never show the metal right, especially with silver—it always picks up whatever color is around it. But yeah, the sleep thing was definitely less than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. After the show, especially, I felt kind of lit up, like bigger than myself for a bit. Like everything I’d made was finally clicking and people were seeing it the right way.\n\nAnd then it would flip really fast. If someone asked me to keep normal shop hours or, you know, slow down and not act like the whole place should revolve around whatever idea I had, I’d get sharp with them. Snappy. Even if they weren’t really doing anything wrong. So it wasn’t just happy exactly… it was like happy and electric, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel more confident or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Especially after I won that ribbon, it was like, okay, see, I’ve been right this whole time. I felt like I’d finally proved I was on another level.\n\nI remember looking at the custom orders and thinking I could turn every single one into some showpiece, like something no one else around here could even touch. Which… I mean, some of the local work really is kind of lazy, people just slap a stone in a setting and call it done—but I guess I was feeling more important than usual, yeah.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot when I’m explaining a piece anyway, like if someone asks about a setting I’ll get into why I used a certain prong shape or why the stone sits better lower, and then suddenly I’m talking about polishing compounds or whatever.\n\nSo it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “much more.” People have told me I get going and don’t really stop, but I don’t know if that was those periods specifically or just… me being wound up about the work.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, were you taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I kind of… started trying to spin up everything at once. I was lining up new commissions, messaging old customers like, “Hey, I’ve got new ideas if you want something made,” and then I had this whole plan for a website.\n\nAnd not just a basic website, either. I was thinking it needed sections, photos, maybe a story behind each piece—because people don’t understand how much goes into the metalwork, you know? Like with Appalachian stuff, the patterns in old quilts and rail lines and creek stones, there’s a whole language there.\n\nSo yeah, I was sketching this whole Appalachian collection at the same time, and it felt like I had to get all of it moving right then, before the idea cooled off or somebody else did something similar.\n\nDoctor: During one of those wired periods, did you ever do something that got you into serious trouble at work, financially, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. It was one of those pieces where the customer had been really specific, and I had all these ideas for making it better, and then I was chasing three other things at the same time. My records got all mixed up too—orders, notes, deposits, stone sizes—just scattered. I usually keep that stuff pretty tight, because if you mix up a stone measurement by even a little, the whole setting’s wrong.\n\nI actually had to stop taking new work for now, which feels embarrassing. My family’s been checking on me more too, like calling and stopping by, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I kept telling them I was just busy, but… yeah, it was more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever hear or see things that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing a voice talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes when I’m really locked into a piece, I’ll notice tiny things other people miss—like a little flash in a stone or how the light catches an edge wrong—and I can get kind of fixated on it. My brother will be like, “It looks fine,” and I’m still staring at it under the lamp. But that’s not really hearing or seeing something, I guess. So… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did those wired periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Um… the main one was about two weeks, I think. It started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, which was a big deal for me—like, I kept replaying people coming by the table and asking about the pieces, and I had all these ideas after that.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking days in a row, it wasn’t just a weekend or a couple late nights. It was probably around two weeks where I felt really wired and kind of couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I was hospitalized for it. I mean, people were definitely worried, and there was a lot going on with work and my family hovering more than usual, but I didn’t end up in the hospital.\n\nI remember thinking at the time that if I could just get the shop cleaned up and sort the orders, it would all kind of settle down. The little bins on my bench were a mess—gold scraps in the wrong tray, invoices under polishing cloths, just… ridiculous. But no, not hospitalized.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… these past couple weeks, I think so. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the shine’s gone out of everything.\n\nIt’s weird because with jewelry, I’m always looking for shine, you know? Like even a dull stone can come back if you polish it right, or turn it a little under the light. But lately I’ll sit in the truck before opening the shop and just… I’ve cried there before going in. Then I wipe my face and try to act normal because customers don’t want to see that.\n\nSo, yeah. It’s been pretty much every day, most of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, which was a big deal for me—like, I kept replaying people coming by the table and asking about the pieces, and I had all these ideas after that.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking days in a row, it wasn’t just a weekend or a couple late nights. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I haven’t made a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some… specific way in my head.

But I do keep getting these thoughts that maybe my family would be better off if I wasn’t around. And sometimes I think about my clients too, which probably sounds strange, but like… people trust me with engagement rings, heirlooms, little repairs that matter to them, and lately I just feel like I’m messing everything up by existing near it.

It scares me how often it comes up, honestly. Not like I’m about to do something right this second, but the thought keeps showing up, and I don’t like that it feels so familiar.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.1, F31.9, F90.0, F41.1, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what really made me come in is… I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like at work, at the bench, I’ll start answering a customer email, then I notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside and I’m looking up, and then suddenly I’ve lost track of the whole work order.\n\nAnd jewelry work, you know, it’s not really something where you can just be halfway there—one little mistake and the piece is off, or the customer gets upset. My boss has this old radio near the polishing area too, and sometimes that just pulls me off completely. But yeah… mostly it’s that my attention is all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I wasn’t exactly the kid sitting there loving worksheets, but who was? I did okay enough to get through.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, because back then I was more worried about shop class and hanging around after school, and my mom used to say I’d “forget my head if it wasn’t attached,” but she said that about everything—keys, lunch, chores. I don’t know if that’s the same kind of attention problem you mean.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, were you often distracted or forgetful at school or at home?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Like, before 12… that’s kind of fuzzy. I remember forgetting stuff here and there, like homework or my jacket, but I don’t know if it was “often” or just normal kid stuff.\n\nAt home my mom would get on me about chores, but she got on my brother too, so I don’t know. And school feels like a blur now—teachers, lockers, cafeteria pizza, all that. I can’t really say for sure that it was a big problem back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I think everybody worries, right? Like bills, work, whether a customer’s gonna come back mad because a clasp isn’t sitting right—well, not mad, but you know, people can be picky with jewelry.\n\nBut when you say “even when there isn’t a problem,” I’m not sure. I don’t really sit down and label it as worrying. Sometimes my head’s just busy, and I’ll be thinking about three random things, like whether I locked the case at work or if I need gas, then I remember some guy from high school I haven’t talked to in years. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. There’ve been times where I felt really wired for a few days, like I just couldn’t settle down.\n\nAt work, especially, I couldn’t sit still at the bench. I’d start sketching out a new watch case, then suddenly I’d be emailing suppliers, then I’d decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them even after closing. Which is funny because those trays are never right anyway—people move things around all day and then I’m the one fixing it.\n\nBut yeah, it wasn’t just normal busy. It was like I had to keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… during those times I’d sleep way less. Like maybe two or three hours, and then I’d pop up feeling weirdly clear, like I was ready to get started.\n\nI remember getting up before sunrise thinking I should cut stones or post new design ideas online, even though normally I’d be dragging if I slept that little. And then I’d get sidetracked looking at lighting for photos because the pictures never show the metal right, especially with silver—it always picks up whatever color is around it. But yeah, the sleep thing was definitely less than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. After the show, especially, I felt kind of lit up, like bigger than myself for a bit. Like everything I’d made was finally clicking and people were seeing it the right way.\n\nAnd then it would flip really fast. If someone asked me to keep normal shop hours or, you know, slow down and not act like the whole place should revolve around whatever idea I had, I’d get sharp with them. Snappy. Even if they weren’t really doing anything wrong. So it wasn’t just happy exactly… it was like happy and electric, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel more confident or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Especially after I won that ribbon, it was like, okay, see, I’ve been right this whole time. I felt like I’d finally proved I was on another level.\n\nI remember looking at the custom orders and thinking I could turn every single one into some showpiece, like something no one else around here could even touch. Which… I mean, some of the local work really is kind of lazy, people just slap a stone in a setting and call it done—but I guess I was feeling more important than usual, yeah.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot when I’m explaining a piece anyway, like if someone asks about a setting I’ll get into why I used a certain prong shape or why the stone sits better lower, and then suddenly I’m talking about polishing compounds or whatever.\n\nSo it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “much more.” People have told me I get going and don’t really stop, but I don’t know if that was those periods specifically or just… me being wound up about the work.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, were you taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I kind of… started trying to spin up everything at once. I was lining up new commissions, messaging old customers like, “Hey, I’ve got new ideas if you want something made,” and then I had this whole plan for a website.\n\nAnd not just a basic website, either. I was thinking it needed sections, photos, maybe a story behind each piece—because people don’t understand how much goes into the metalwork, you know? Like with Appalachian stuff, the patterns in old quilts and rail lines and creek stones, there’s a whole language there.\n\nSo yeah, I was sketching this whole Appalachian collection at the same time, and it felt like I had to get all of it moving right then, before the idea cooled off or somebody else did something similar.\n\nDoctor: During one of those wired periods, did you ever do something that got you into serious trouble at work, financially, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. It was one of those pieces where the customer had been really specific, and I had all these ideas for making it better, and then I was chasing three other things at the same time. My records got all mixed up too—orders, notes, deposits, stone sizes—just scattered. I usually keep that stuff pretty tight, because if you mix up a stone measurement by even a little, the whole setting’s wrong.\n\nI actually had to stop taking new work for now, which feels embarrassing. My family’s been checking on me more too, like calling and stopping by, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I kept telling them I was just busy, but… yeah, it was more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever hear or see things that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing a voice talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes when I’m really locked into a piece, I’ll notice tiny things other people miss—like a little flash in a stone or how the light catches an edge wrong—and I can get kind of fixated on it. My brother will be like, “It looks fine,” and I’m still staring at it under the lamp. But that’s not really hearing or seeing something, I guess. So… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did those wired periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Um… the main one was about two weeks, I think. It started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, which was a big deal for me—like, I kept replaying people coming by the table and asking about the pieces, and I had all these ideas after that.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking days in a row, it wasn’t just a weekend or a couple late nights. It was probably around two weeks where I felt really wired and kind of couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I was hospitalized for it. I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, with the jewelry work, I still go in and do it because it’s my shop and people are waiting on things, but whether I enjoy it… I don’t know.

Like with Appalachian stuff, the patterns in old quilts and rail lines and creek stones, there’s a whole language there.\n\nSo yeah, I was sketching this whole Appalachian collection at the same time, and it felt like I had to get all of it moving right then, before the idea cooled off or somebody else did something similar.\n\nDoctor: During one of those wired periods, did you ever do something that got you into serious trouble at work, financially, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. I remember forgetting stuff here and there, like homework or my jacket, but I don’t know if it was “often” or just normal kid stuff.\n\nAt home my mom would get on me about chores, but she got on my brother too, so I don’t know. And school feels like a blur now—teachers, lockers, cafeteria pizza, all that. I can’t really say for sure that it was a big problem back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I think everybody worries, right? Like bills, work, whether a customer’s gonna come back mad because a clasp isn’t sitting right—well, not mad, but you know, people can be picky with jewelry.\n\nBut when you say “even when there isn’t a problem,” I’m not sure. I don’t really sit down and label it as worrying. Sometimes my head’s just busy, and I’ll be thinking about three random things, like whether I locked the case at work or if I need gas, then I remember some guy from high school I haven’t talked to in years. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or wired for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. There’ve been times where I felt really wired for a few days, like I just couldn’t settle down.\n\nAt work, especially, I couldn’t sit still at the bench. I’d start sketching out a new watch case, then suddenly I’d be emailing suppliers, then I’d decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them even after closing. Which is funny because those trays are never right anyway—people move things around all day and then I’m the one fixing it.\n\nBut yeah, it wasn’t just normal busy. It was like I had to keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… during those times I’d sleep way less. Like maybe two or three hours, and then I’d pop up feeling weirdly clear, like I was ready to get started.\n\nI remember getting up before sunrise thinking I should cut stones or post new design ideas online, even though normally I’d be dragging if I slept that little. And then I’d get sidetracked looking at lighting for photos because the pictures never show the metal right, especially with silver—it always picks up whatever color is around it. But yeah, the sleep thing was definitely less than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. After the show, especially, I felt kind of lit up, like bigger than myself for a bit. Like everything I’d made was finally clicking and people were seeing it the right way.\n\nAnd then it would flip really fast. If someone asked me to keep normal shop hours or, you know, slow down and not act like the whole place should revolve around whatever idea I had, I’d get sharp with them. Snappy. Even if they weren’t really doing anything wrong. So it wasn’t just happy exactly… it was like happy and electric, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you feel more confident or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Especially after I won that ribbon, it was like, okay, see, I’ve been right this whole time. I felt like I’d finally proved I was on another level.\n\nI remember looking at the custom orders and thinking I could turn every single one into some showpiece, like something no one else around here could even touch. Which… I mean, some of the local work really is kind of lazy, people just slap a stone in a setting and call it done—but I guess I was feeling more important than usual, yeah.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I talk a lot when I’m explaining a piece anyway, like if someone asks about a setting I’ll get into why I used a certain prong shape or why the stone sits better lower, and then suddenly I’m talking about polishing compounds or whatever.\n\nSo it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “much more.” People have told me I get going and don’t really stop, but I don’t know if that was those periods specifically or just… me being wound up about the work.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, were you taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I kind of… started trying to spin up everything at once. I was lining up new commissions, messaging old customers like, “Hey, I’ve got new ideas if you want something made,” and then I had this whole plan for a website.\n\nAnd not just a basic website, either. I was thinking it needed sections, photos, maybe a story behind each piece—because people don’t understand how much goes into the metalwork, you know? Like with Appalachian stuff, the patterns in old quilts and rail lines and creek stones, there’s a whole language there.\n\nSo yeah, I was sketching this whole Appalachian collection at the same time, and it felt like I had to get all of it moving right then, before the idea cooled off or somebody else did something similar.\n\nDoctor: During one of those wired periods, did you ever do something that got you into serious trouble at work, financially, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did get me in trouble, I guess. Not like police trouble or anything, but work-wise, yeah.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which was… awful. It was one of those pieces where the customer had been really specific, and I had all these ideas for making it better, and then I was chasing three other things at the same time. My records got all mixed up too—orders, notes, deposits, stone sizes—just scattered. I usually keep that stuff pretty tight, because if you mix up a stone measurement by even a little, the whole setting’s wrong.\n\nI actually had to stop taking new work for now, which feels embarrassing. My family’s been checking on me more too, like calling and stopping by, because they could tell I’d gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I kept telling them I was just busy, but… yeah, it was more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those wired periods, did you ever hear or see things that other people didn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing a voice talking to me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes when I’m really locked into a piece, I’ll notice tiny things other people miss—like a little flash in a stone or how the light catches an edge wrong—and I can get kind of fixated on it. My brother will be like, “It looks fine,” and I’m still staring at it under the lamp. But that’s not really hearing or seeing something, I guess. So… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did those wired periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Um… the main one was about two weeks, I think. It started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, which was a big deal for me—like, I kept replaying people coming by the table and asking about the pieces, and I had all these ideas after that.\n\nBut yeah, if you’re asking days in a row, it wasn’t just a weekend or a couple late nights. It was probably around two weeks where I felt really wired and kind of couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period, were you ever hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I was hospitalized for it. I mean, people were definitely worried, and there was a lot going on with work and my family hovering more than usual, but I didn’t end up in the hospital.\n\nI remember thinking at the time that if I could just get the shop cleaned up and sort the orders, it would all kind of settle down. The little bins on my bench were a mess—gold scraps in the wrong tray, invoices under polishing cloths, just… ridiculous. But no, not hospitalized.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… these past couple weeks, I think so. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the shine’s gone out of everything.\n\nIt’s weird because with jewelry, I’m always looking for shine, you know? Like even a dull stone can come back if you polish it right, or turn it a little under the light. But lately I’ll sit in the truck before opening the shop and just… I’ve cried there before going in. Then I wipe my face and try to act normal because customers don’t want to see that.\n\nSo, yeah. It’s been pretty much every day, most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t made a plan or anything like that. I don’t have some… specific way in my head.\n\nBut I do keep getting these thoughts that maybe my family would be better off if I wasn’t around. And sometimes I think about my clients too, which probably sounds strange, but like… people trust me with engagement rings, heirlooms, little repairs that matter to them, and lately I just feel like I’m messing everything up by existing near it.\n\nIt scares me how often it comes up, honestly. Not like I’m about to do something right this second, but the thought keeps showing up, and I don’t like that it feels so familiar.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like your jewelry work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, with the jewelry work, I still go in and do it because it’s my shop and people are waiting on things, but whether I enjoy it… I don’t know.\n\nSometimes I’ll look at a piece and think, “This should be satisfying,” like setting a stone cleanly or getting a ring polished just right. Usually that feeling is there. Lately it’s harder to tell. I get distracted by stupid things too, like whether the display lights are making the silver look too cold, or if I should’ve ordered different velvet trays—those black ones show every speck of dust, which drives me nuts.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know if it’s lost interest or just everything feeling kind of muted."
